Vayudristi’s Internet of Things (IoT) concept is promising. The data collected can be leveraged for various use cases, but the required investment is significant.
As data volume grows, they will need substantial computing and storage infrastructure, which can be very expensive—especially since hosting it in the cloud is the only viable option. Physical infrastructure isn’t practical or cost-effective at scale.

The founder appears smart—perhaps he has ideas for scaling. I would recommend pitching the concept to Middle Eastern investors, who not only have the capital but are also actively investing in infrastructure and technology.

As for the last individual, Chukul, he seems questionable. If he’s developing a trading algorithm, why is he selling access to members instead of trading with it himself to identify market anomalies and earn profits?
If his company is offering trading advice under the promise of profit, the Nepal Stock Exchange should consider auditing them to ensure regulatory compliance and protect retail investors.

“Shark Tank should be a platform that uplifts innovation and entrepreneurship from all corners of the country, regardless of region or background. But when poor or marginalized people—like the entrepreneur from the far-west—are rejected without fair consideration, while others with similar ideas are heavily supported, it raises serious questions about fairness and equality. Business should be judged on potential and passion, not on where someone comes from or how wealthy they look. Let’s not divide dreams—let’s support all Nepalis equally. This kind of bias kills hope and trust in such platforms.”

This episode of Nepal Shark Tank could have been far more impactful if the Sharks had chosen to invest in the traditional handicraft business. The presentation wasn’t just about profit — it was about preserving and promoting our Nepali heritage, identity, and craftsmanship.
Unlike many Westernized business ideas that tend to dominate such platforms, this was a purely Nepali initiative, deeply rooted in our culture and tradition. Supporting it would not only have uplifted a local entrepreneur but also sent a strong message to the world — that Nepal is proud of its roots and capable of turning its traditions into globally recognized brands.
An investment here could have added true value to the idea of a “Nepali” Shark Tank — a platform that champions our uniqueness, rather than chasing trends. Sometimes, it’s not just about returns, but about long-term cultural impact

The car cleaning business is awesome—I see huge growth potential.
The map business, on the other hand, is a flop because it’s an operationally and capital-intensive business. The owner knows this, which is why he gave up 40% equity.
Local data storage requires massive capital investment in infrastructure and data centers—it’s simply not feasible.
Tracking coordinates from satellites is a more feasible approach.
Google can collaborate with local people to obtain geological data from the Nepal government. I’m confident their AI models will be able to process and accurately locate complex geographic information.
I’m starting to feel that the sharks don’t really understand the technology. They’re getting excited by marketing buzzwords like API, AI, and “competing with Google,” without grasping the underlying challenges.

As a viewer and a potential customer, I felt something was missing in this episode. The Sharks never asked about the MRP of the product or whether average Nepali customers can actually afford it. In Nepal, where many people still struggle financially, pricing plays a huge role in success.

Before talking about profits and scale, shouldn’t we ask: "Is this product affordable to the people we are trying to serve?" Without understanding the customer base and price sensitivity, how can we expect a business to be truly sustainable?

I hope future episodes focus more on real market fit, affordability, and value for Nepali consumers, not just branding and margins.

This story is from Shark Tank Nepal, episode 06,  and point to the study of how to handle shark and play with them, as Alchi Pasal founder Manish Shrestha  and his wife, Kriti Shakya, asked 1.5 crores for 25% equity in a board game with a valuation of 6 crores, with revenue in last year, 2024, of 60 lakhs, with a margin of 40%, profit, hero product 'samrajya' price 1500, other costs as momo 800 and other 600, all in nepali money. 
here shark offered by Cabinet Sharestha as shark as NPR 1.5 CRORES for 40 % equity and valuation will be 3.75 crores, 
and then founder went out and came up with idea to counter offer as NPR 1 crores for 25% equity + 50 lakhs debt with valuation 4 crores
again shark said counteroffer as NPR 1 crores for 25% equity + 50 lakhs debt with  profit sharing  and valuation 4 crores, then founder agree and deal was closed with happy
but other shark said it was very smart played  by  the founder, as h took 1.5 crores money , saved 40% equity as only gave 25% with saving 15% equity and got good valuation not down, 
so please tell first what is this  50 lakhs debt with and different between and 50 lakhs debt with  profit sharing , what is point from both shark view and founder and in which case it is good both or oneside 
and really is this smart to play with shark to save equity and valuation and still able to convince shark and win win way, after this please give such real exmple of such way trick and way to play it and also if such done then story in very simple way...

I don’t know why the sharks are using so much English... it’s totally fine to use technical terms that don’t have proper Nepali alternatives, but why have full conversations in English? What’s the point of calling the show “Shark Tank Nepal” if English is being used more than Nepali? There are lots of viewers who may not be fluent in English but are genuinely interested in business—or just casual viewers who won’t understand much if the whole episode is in English.

Note: Please don’t take this as a hate comment. It’s just a suggestion from a regular viewer. The show is really doing great, and I hope to see some improvement in the language usage in future episodes. 🙌

Kudos for the team of Shark Tank Nepal. However, until 4th episode I found the most repetitive way of opting out is " This is not my area of expertise". Entrepreneur's basic feature is creativity, innovation and risk taking. And if the offer aligns with their sector they opt out as a fear of competition, threat of substitute, conflict of interest. Primary objective of Shark Tank is to fund the innovation where Sharks seek for diverse investment opportunities. That is exactly not happening here till the fourth episode. Instead of saying "not my area of expertise" why not reject them with a constructive suggestion for their business pointing out the gaps they see.
 
Having said that, launching Shark Tank Nepal here is Nepal shows that our people is in the right path of entrepreneurship.

The first business was impressive—I see significant growth potential in Kiwi. The last SaaS application appears to be growing only within Nepal, as leading international players are not interested in gaining market share there due to low profit margins and currency volatility. Most SaaS applications will likely become obsolete within 5 to 10 years, as agentic AI systems are expected to replace them. As mentioned, running advanced AI models in Nepal is not feasible because the cost of operating even basic models is too high. While simple analytics through software optimization is possible, large-scale data processing using predictive analysis or Bayesian models will be extremely difficult due to infrastructure and cost limitations. The sharks are not deeply knowledgeable in advanced technologies, which is why they tend to get excited about nearly all the software applications pitched so far. I don’t see substantial long-term growth potential in Veda Software.

False information on natural preservatives. Here's the verified information:

🧪 Euxyl PE 9010
Official INCI listing confirms it's a blend of Phenoxyethanol (90%) and Ethylhexylglycerin (10%)—both synthetic ingredients 
Proven efficacy via broad-spectrum antimicrobial testing; fully synthetic blend 

🧪 Euxyl K 913
Although proprietary, Euxyl K 913 is part of the Euxyl K line (K 900, K 903), all of which feature phenoxyethanol or benzyl alcohol plus synthetic boosters like ethylhexylglycerin or dehydroacetic acid 
These components are all industrially synthesized, not plant-derived.

🧪 Geogard Ultra
Composition detailed in technical datasheets: ~70–80% gluconolactone (from fermented, non‑GMO corn), ~22–28% sodium benzoate (synthetic), with trace calcium gluconate 
Humblebee and Me
Certified by ECOCERT, COSMOS, NATRUE, Soil Association—recognized as a natural-leaning but not fully natural preservatives 


✅ Final Verification Table
Preservative	Composition	100% Natural?
Euxyl PE 9010	Phenoxyethanol + Ethylhexylglycerin	❌ No
Euxyl K 913	Phenoxyethanol (or Benzyl Alcohol) + synthetics	❌ No
Geogard Ultra	Gluconolactone (natural) + Sodium Benzoate (synthetic)	❌ No

Great Opportunities For Startup Companies. Shark Tank Nepal plays a transformative role in empowering entrepreneurs by providing them with a unique platform to pitch their business ideas directly to potential investors i.e. Sharks. It not only opens doors to crucial funding and expert mentorship but also helps startups gain nationwide visibility, enhancing their credibility and market reach. At the same time, the show offers engaging and inspiring content to viewers, blending real-life innovation with entertainment. By showcasing entrepreneurial journeys, business challenges, and creative problem-solving, Shark Tank Nepal motivates aspiring entrepreneurs while educating the public on the dynamics of investment and business growth.
After watching this show, those who aspire to start a business but haven’t been able to bring their ideas to life due to lack of sufficient capital will begin to believe that they will receive support through one medium or another.

29:30 Non of the preservatives she mentioned are natural. Wow!

Euxyl® PE (commonly referring to Euxyl PE 9010) is a synthetic preservative system, not natural—but it’s often considered a "gentler" or "clean-label" friendly option, especially in paraben-free or phenol-free formulations.

Geogard Ultra is a patented, synergistic blend of:
Gluconolactone (a naturally-derived polyhydroxy acid from fermented glucose)
Sodium benzoate (a classic preservative, technically synthetic)
Trace calcium gluconate as a processing aid

Euxyl K 913 is a strong, reliable preservative system—
excellent for ensuring safety and shelf-life in a range of cosmetic formulations.
Not a natural preservative, though suitable for “clean” product positioning.
If you're aiming for fully natural or organic labeling, options like Geogard Ultra, Leucidal, or Olivem PureLytica may be more appropriate.

What many viewers don’t realize is that a large number of deals on Shark Tank fall apart after filming. Once the cameras stop rolling, the sharks begin a process called due diligence, where they verify the business’s financials, claims, and background. If anything seems off, the deal is quietly canceled behind the scenes.
But for many entrepreneurs, getting on Shark Tank isn’t just about the investment. It’s about publicity. Even without a deal, a few minutes of national TV exposure can bring in thousands of new customers, sometimes far more valuable than the money itself.

Uttam Kafle sir i want to suggest you something. Instead of buying a refurbished or repaired phone you can provide a plarform or experience center. For example I have some phone and I want to sell it in the second hand market. You pick the phone do all the repairing and maintanance in the my own cost and you provide better plarform like website and app with bigger network and traffic. And when the phone gets sold you and get some commission and platform fee. In your model scaling seems too big of a problem. For example if you buy 50 iphones and if 10 of them doesn't gets sold for year then most of your working capital will be blocked. 
I have analyzed hundreds of business and i see such business strugling with inventory, working capital and Distressed Inventory . This dead stock of inventory can be too problematic when it gets scaled. Maybe the business model require little update. There are too many players in the game and most of them are organized.

This might be my first time commenting on YouTube, and while that may not be a big thing, I felt compelled to share my thoughts — as someone who is like-minded and genuinely passionate about business and entrepreneurship.

I truly enjoyed every aspect of the show — from the professionalism of the entire setup to the way emerging entrepreneurs confidently present their business models, ideas, goals, missions, and projections. Equally impressive is how the sharks evaluate, ask critical questions, raise objections, and ultimately make their deals. Everything flows with such structure and purpose — it’s inspiring.

Most importantly, the core message this show delivers is powerful: we can build and grow impactful businesses right here in Nepal. We don’t have to leave the country to chase dreams — we can make things happen at home, contribute to the economy, and create something meaningful for the next generation. The potential is massive.

Hats off to the successful business personalities (the sharks), who are not just investing but empowering — helping the "small fish" become future sharks, all while keeping their investments rooted in Nepal instead of abroad.

Kudos to the entire Shark Tank Nepal team and all the participants for bringing such a valuable and inspiring reality show to life in Nepal.

And finally, to all the aspiring entrepreneurs who may not have secured a deal: Don’t give up. Stay focused. Keep moving forward with a clear vision. Believe in yourself, trust the process, and never stop growing. Your journey has just begun.

When we Nepali people get called sheep they're not wrong. I have an issue about the criticism towards the brand Eletra. First thing I'd like to ask is why don't you go to Colgate and ask "Colgate, why are you accusing? Because as we all have seen them say “9 out of 10 dentists recommend Colgate over any other brand.” Everyone has the right to market their brand as the better one if they have proper claims to back it up.
And I'm shocked with what Hemraj, Anand and Ritu said. As Entrepreneurs, in business one of the first things we learn is to differentiate our products/services from the market which is known as product differentiation the proper meaning for product differentiation is, the process of distinguishing a product or service from others in the market to make it more appealing to a specific target audience. I went through their pitch 2 times and didn't see them claiming they're a "Made in Nepal" company from the starting they've said they are getting it from India. Which is completely normal and nothing's wrong with it since they're not claiming it's made in Nepal. The reason it's fresh and called a startup is because they're in the initial phase. It's not necessary for a new company to have a dedicated R&D team as they are sourcing their products from a company which passes all the legal compliance. When we sell a product like this it's called sourcing through an OEM. The founder of "Boat" Mr. Aman Gupta, did not start boat with a full fledged R&D and in house manufacturing. He relied on OEM from China yet he is there as a shark in a much bigger market than ours. Neither did he have academic or professional background of actually manufacturing the products when he worked in JBL. He worked as the Director of sales. Neither did his partner, Sameer Mehta have a background with it. I'm talking about boat as an example. I understand that skincare is more fragile than a pair of headphones but how many companies in the whole skincare market have the owner's who are actual dermatologists? And what rule/law states you need to be a dermatologist to own a skincare brand? If they have access to the recommendations/consultations from actual dermatologists they need not have a full time dermatologists on their side because they're in a very early stage with a very limited funding. If they had access to millions of rupees they wouldn't come to shark tank on the first place and they'd have all the resources they need. Even if a dermatologist opens a skincare company they will still need a lab they need to send it to regardless of how big of an R&D team they have. If they don't have the lab certification, even a dermatologist's skincare will be good for nothing. If they have the facts and proof to back their claims with proper testing along with products from a licensed skincare manufacturing company, they are not wrong at all. If they have claims to differentiate between them and the rest of the market I 100% believe they have a right to portray that message as loud as possible. And the last thing is that they do not seem to be just accusing they're also making claims with the proof to back it up. 

This is shark tank Nepal where sharks invest in businesses in Nepal and not businesses that only sells products made in Nepal.

The problem isn't in the business it's the way we think but don't analyze or don't look at how everyone is functioning in the rest of the world which is a major reason we are always behind. We are so struck up on what these sharks are saying that we don't tend to look at anything else. It really explains why we are called "bheda" and why our country is number 1 in terms of lowest IQ. I was excited when shark tank was announced and I saw an opportunity which gives hope to the young generation to think outside the box but yet again our minds are just trapping us to think everything we see does not need to be looked at from a practical point of view. The same people with the mindset that says " I wish there could be an environment for young generations to earn and live a quality of life and start a business here in my home country Nepal" are commenting negative things just because they cannot look at things from a practical perspective. 
It's not just the government behind thousands of students moving abroad for better opportunities, its mostly the people with the mindset that anything someone who is famous or someone who may have better knowledge say should not and will not be fact checked and blindly believed and agreed upon. The reason our education system is so backwards is our stupid habit to not being able to question or analyze or fact check what has been said or taught to us.

Sharks are the largest fish in the ocean and eat smaller fish to survive.

Similarly, just as a large net is cast into the ocean with various promises, another net is cast into your newly started business and intellectual field, and your skills and your dreams are swallowed whole by these sharks, which you do not even know. Therefore, do not fall into temptation and gradually develop your skills, arts and ideas on your own.

It is the law of nature that big fish eat small fish. If you survive, you will one day be a big fish yourself.

Shark Tank Nepal is a great initiative for the country’s startup ecosystem, but I feel it still lacks the spark that such a platform demands. Many pitches come across as very traditional and unprepared, with weak presentations that fail to grab attention. In today’s modern world, pitches need to be clear, confident, and creative to leave an impression on both the sharks and the audience.
While Nepal’s market is smaller compared to India or other countries, that shouldn’t stop us from improving the quality of presentations and making the show more dynamic and engaging. With a bit more energy and innovation in how ideas are pitched, Shark Tank Nepal can truly inspire the next generation of entrepreneurs.

As a viewer of Shark Tank Nepal, I felt genuinely disappointed with how the judges handled one particular pitch. The female contestant clearly lacked a solid understanding of core business principles. While she was granted funding, there was little to no constructive guidance offered. In such cases, it’s essential not just to provide capital but also mentorship, strategic advice, and direction. Business growth doesn’t come from money alone it requires knowledge, clarity, and support. Judges have a responsibility to uplift and educate, especially when a contestant shows potential but lacks experience.

aauta humble suggestion I wouldd like to offer is regarding the language used on the show. Since yo aauta Nepali reality show ho, I noticed that the Sharks often communicate in English while interacting with the entrepreneurs. While its understandable in certain contexts, I feel it might be more inclusive and effective if the use of English is minimized during key conversations cuz not only sharks we also want to know something about  the product being discussed and gather some business knowledge from it but sabai Nepali haru might not be fluent in English, and this could unintentionally create misunderstandings or communication barriers especially for aspiring entrepreneurs from diverse backgrounds. So malai k lagxa using more Nepali could make the show feel more relatable and accessible to a wider audience across the country.

In Nepal, there is no easy way for consumers to verify what’s really in the food they eat. Whether it’s packaged snacks, dairy, spices, or restaurant food – we’re forced to blindly trust whatever the seller claims. There’s little or no information about:
	•	Where the food comes from
	•	What ingredients or preservatives are used
	•	Expiry, freshness, or hygiene standards
	•	Independent testing or third-party verification

This is a serious public health issue, especially in urban and semi-urban areas. People unknowingly consume adulterated or low-quality food, risking illness, long-term health damage, and loss of trust in local businesses.

He criticized the proliferation of gambling-centric platforms, the exploitation of unemployed youth through low-wage delivery jobs, and companies such as Boat—led by Aman Gupta—which primarily assemble products imported from China rather than contributing to meaningful domestic manufacturing or innovation. 

Nepal should prioritize inviting individuals who are truly driving innovation and leading transformative initiatives within the startup ecosystem, rather than figures like Aman Gupta—whose company, Boat, is primarily engaged in assembling products imported from China without significant value addition or indigenous innovation.

This is exactly what needs to happen in a country like Nepal, where mainstream conversations are dominated by politics. Traditional media mostly feeds political news, and since federalism, they’ve added local provincial politics to the mix — as if national-level politics weren’t overwhelming enough already. It’s time for a shift. A small change, like introducing a ‘Shark Tank’-style platform, could spark a revolution in how Nepali citizens think. Proper capital allocation can drive real change and scale in society. We need every young person to start talking about entrepreneurship, the private sector, startups, and economic growth. While we may not yet match international standards, the most important thing is that Nepali entrepreneurs are beginning to think about contributing to the nation’s economic development.

There are no words in the dictionary to describe how i'm felling while watching SHARK TANK NEPAL.  Truly thankful to the entire team and the Sharks. I'm waiting impatiently for the next episode.
This is exactly what needs to happen in a country like Nepal, where mainstream conversations are dominated by politics. Traditional media mostly feeds political news, and since federalism, they’ve added local provincial politics to the mix — as if national-level politics weren’t overwhelming enough already. It’s time for a shift. A small change, like introducing a ‘Shark Tank’-style platform, could spark a revolution in how Nepali citizens think. Proper capital allocation can drive real change and scale in society. We need every young person to start talking about entrepreneurship, the private sector, startups, and economic growth. While we may not yet match international standards, the most important thing is that Nepali entrepreneurs are beginning to think about contributing to the nation’s economic development.
